result. **That was deliberate**: DF-1 proved an empty vendor response can also mean _our_ request is
malformed, so returning an empty canonical object would turn that class of bug into a
plausible-looking cached answer. Request-shape assertions (`TC-UNIT-07`, the Solana case) are where
that gets caught; a runtime heuristic cannot.

Mechanics worth knowing before touching this:

- `CacheStore.set()` gained an optional `ttlSecondsOverride` — **one** call site uses it. Without
  it a negative would inherit the capability TTL (3600s for `entity.labels`).
- Expiry is checked against the entry's own embedded `expiresAtMs`, NOT the store's `ageMs`: the
  two-level store promotes cold hits into its hot layer using `ttlFor(capability)`, which is the
  wrong number for a negative.
- Cached reasons are surfaced with a `[cached negative]` suffix, so a just-fixed request bug does
  not look unfixed for a minute.

**Q-4 — `token.risk` now reads the sub-call it always paid for.**

`/tgm/token-information` was fetched, charged (1cr of 6) and discarded. Two options were open —
drop it or consume it — and the recorded fixtures decided it at zero credits: `/tgm/indicators`'
own `token_info` carries **three** fields, `/tgm/token-information` carries **~20**. R-43's
"(метаданные)" was right; the implementation was wrong. `TokenRiskScore` gained `deploymentDate`
(token age — kept as the vendor's raw string, never parsed: no timezone in the format),
`liquidityUsd`, `totalHolders`, `fdvUsd`, `circulatingSupply`, `totalSupply`, `name`, `symbol`.
`logo`/`website`/socials deliberately excluded — no risk signal, needless injection surface.

The two sub-responses are not equal in weight: a malformed `indicators` body stays fatal (it is the
substance), a malformed `token-information` body degrades to fields-absent (it is enrichment, and
throwing would re-enter the L-1 class on an already-paid 6cr call).

Price is unchanged at 6cr. This did not make the capability cheaper — it made the 6cr honest.

## `/vdd-multi` cycle 5 (2026-07-26) — the widening's own defects: coverage that over-promised, and labels that lied

The review of TASK-006 found four High defects, all introduced by that task. Each is a variant of
one mistake: **widening the chain set touched what the engine ADVERTISES without touching what it
can DO.**

**H-1 — `chainSupport()` was widened to Nansen's real coverage; the transport was not.**
Task 006-9 read 17/25/25 chains out of the committed OpenAPI spec, but `endpoints.ts` still typed
`chain` as `'ethereum' | 'solana'` and `index.ts` still sent OUR slug. So `token.risk({chain:'bsc'})`
passed the coverage gate, reached `fetch()`, and threw — and `registry.ts` turned that into
`CapabilityUnavailableError`, the class that means "retry". A permanent condition wearing a
retryable error's clothes: the agent loops forever, and on a paid route each loop is another
budget-gate entry. (Money was never at risk — the throw precedes `ensureBudget()`.)

The transport was widened rather than the predicate narrowed, because narrowing would have thrown
away the task's deliverable. Three consequences worth remembering:

- `NansenChain` (a literal union) became `NansenChainRef = {token, family}`. `token` is the
  VENDOR's chain name from `vendors.nansen` (`bnb`, not `bsc`) and is the only thing that goes on
  the wire; `family` is ours and decides `token_address` casing.
- The DF-1 casing rule is now keyed on **family**, not chain name — it was never per-chain, it was
  per-encoding: hex is case-insensitive (fold it, as the vendor's column requires), base58/bech32
  are not (folding corrupts the address). The `never` guard moved to `ChainFamily`, so a new family
  is still a compile error.
- `resolveVendorChain()` is now the single statement of coverage that both `chainSupport()` and
  `fetch()` read. It throws `CapabilityNotCoveredOnChainError`, and `registry.ts` **rethrows that
  class instead of folding it into `CapabilityUnavailableError`** — the error class is the fix.

`test/nansen-transport-coverage.test.ts` holds the invariant the review asked for: every slug the
matrix advertises must be a slug `fetch()` can build a request for. It is derived from the matrix,
so it cannot go stale the way a hand-listed set would.

**M-7, fixed alongside it.** `NANSEN_EXHAUSTIVE_LABELS_CHAINS` had been generated, documented as
refusing "before the 100cr escalation", and read by nobody: `chainSupport()` cannot see
`args.exhaustive`. It is now enforced in `validateArgs()`, before `ensureBudget()`. A money guard
that lives only in a comment is worse than none — it reads as covered and discourages writing the
real one.

**H-3 + M-1 — the balance label. Fix the source before the consumer.**
`rpc-evm.normalize()` hardcoded `symbol: 'ETH', decimals: 18`. True while the route was
ethereum-only; wrong for 18 chains after 006-8. But the obvious fix — "read `chain.nativeSymbol`" —
was ALSO wrong: the generator took DeFiLlama's `tokenSymbol`, which is the chain's _listed_ token,
so `arbitrum → ARB`, `op-mainnet → OP`, `gnosis → GNO`. All three pay gas in ETH/xDAI.

So M-1 landed first: `nativeSymbol` now prefers the EIP-155 registry's `nativeCurrency.symbol`
(the gas token by definition) and falls back to DeFiLlama only outside that registry, where it is
right (`solana → SOL`). 63 rows changed. A new `nativeDecimals` column joins it — 29 chains in the
EIP catalog are not 18-decimal, so the second literal was the same latent bug.

That flip surfaced a PRE-EXISTING defect it would otherwise have inherited: **two rows were joined
to a TESTNET entry of the EIP-155 catalog.** `hyperliquid-l1` is chainId 999, which chainid.network
knows as "Wanchain Testnet" — so the row was taking `WAN` as its gas symbol, `twan` as an alias,
and `gwan-ssl.wandevs.org` as an RPC candidate (the same wrong-chain host rejected by hand during
the 006-8 curation, whose automated liveness check passed because that endpoint really does answer
chainId 999). Testnet rows are now excluded from the EIP index entirely: our base list is
DeFiLlama's mainnet catalog, so a testnet match is wrong by construction, and no EIP data beats
another chain's EIP data.

`rpc-evm` now requires `nativeSymbol`/`nativeDecimals` in `servesChain()` — the one predicate both
`chainSupport()` and `extractFetchArgs()` call, for the same reason as H-1.

**M-3 — `rpcHosts: []` was an SSRF-adjacent hole.** An empty array passed both `chainSupport()`
(`!== null`) and the load schema, then `chainHosts.length > 0 ? … : HOSTS` sent the request to
ETHEREUM's endpoints — another chain's balance, cached and schema-valid. `[]` is exactly what a
curator writes for "looked, found none". Now `.min(1)` at load, and the fallback is gone: the
comment next to it already claimed the branch was unreachable, which is how it survived review.

**H-2 — `.max(64)` does not short-circuit `superRefine` in zod 4.** `too_big` is raised with
`continue: true`, so the refinement ran on strings of any length and walked ~1300 Levenshtein
candidates. Measured: 64 chars → 9.8 ms, 20 000 → 416 ms, linear. The guard is now the first line
of the refinement, plus a bounded Levenshtein with an O(1) length-difference bail and precomputed
candidate keys. The project had already closed this exact class in `chain/address.ts`
(`MAX_DECODABLE_ADDRESS_LENGTH` before an O(n²) base58 decode) and did not carry the lesson across.

**H-4 — the shipped registry is built once.** `loadChainRegistry()` memoizes the no-deps path; an
injected `data` is still built fresh every call. Not the mutable singleton §8 forbids — a memoized
pure function of a build constant. The handler half matters more than the cost: canonicalizing
against the shipped snapshot while the gate resolves against `ctx.registry` is two dictionaries for
one request.

**Smaller ones, same theme.** `isCovered()` now excludes deprecated chains (M-5 — the gate said
covered while every list said otherwise); `dune.chainSupport` returns false (L-10 — coverage does
not consult `isAvailable()`, so a permanently-unavailable adapter was advertising `token.holders`);
CoinGecko encodes its path segments (M-4 — `bitcoin` has no address validator, so
`'../../../simple/price?ids=x'` rewrote the request path with our API key attached); vendor-authored
symbols/names are truncated in `normalize()` with schema `.max()` as backstops (M-6 — truncate,
never reject, per the cycle-2 lesson: a parse throw discards a response already paid for);
`chain.tvl` shares one `/v2/chains` download across chains (L-9 — the engine cache is keyed per
chain and cannot deduplicate it).

## `/vdd-multi` cycle 6 (2026-07-27) — reviewing the fixes, and the trigger the task pulled without noticing

The second pass was aimed at cycle 5's own fixes — the freshest, least-reviewed code in the branch.
Two of the three top findings were about that code, one of them a straight regression. Read that as
a fact about review economics, not about cycle 5: **a fix is code, and code that has been reviewed
once has been reviewed once.**

**C-1 (Critical) — the widening opened PAID routes onto families we cannot validate addresses for.**
Seven Nansen-covered chains (`tron`, `ton`, `near`, `sui`, `starknet`, `sei`, `bitcoin`) are
`family: 'other'`, where `isValidAddress` accepts any 1–128-character string and `normalizeAddress`
returns its input verbatim. Three consequences at once, all costing money:

- a garbage `tokenAddress` passed the schema, passed the coverage gate and reserved credits — and
  every distinct garbage string is a distinct `deriveArgsHash`, so neither cache bounded the spend;
- two case variants of one REAL address were two cache entries, i.e. two charges;
- the DF-1 casing rule is unknown for those chains, so a legitimate call could return an empty body
  after payment, throw in `normalize`, and be paid for again on retry.

`chain/address.ts` had already written the rule down: a family without a validator is fine "until a
PAID provider appears on such a family, because only then does the split start costing money
(OQ-1)". TASK-006 pulled that trigger and nobody revisited OQ-1. The condition was stated correctly
and never checked — which is a more useful lesson than the bug.

Now `hasAddressValidator(family)` states the policy once, and `nansenServesChain()` requires it.
That predicate is read by `chainSupport`, by the transport, and by the error's "available on" list —
one statement, three readers, which is the shape cycle 5's H-1 was missing. **Stated cost:**
`entity.labels` 25→18, `token.risk` 24→18, `smart-money.flows` 17→16. `sei` is the strongest
candidate to come back (EVM-addressed in practice, filed under `other` only because no EIP-155
chain id joined) — but re-classifying it needs evidence, so it waits.

**H-1 — the twin nobody looked at.** Cycle 5 hardened `rpc-evm` (per-chain hosts, registry-derived
symbol/decimals) and left `rpc-solana` untouched: it advertised coverage for ANY svm chain with a
curated host while sending every request to `api.mainnet-beta.solana.com` and labelling the result
`SOL`/9. Latent today (solana is the only such row) and guaranteed the day a second SVM chain is
curated — which this branch exists to make a data edit. Its docstring even claimed it was "correct
by construction if another ever appears". **When a fix is about a class of defect, grep for the
class.**

`solana`'s row gained a curated `nativeDecimals: 9` — not a new claim, just the literal that had
been sitting in the adapter since M1, moved into data where the registry can own it.

**H-2 — a regression cycle 5 introduced.** Widening the transport to send Nansen's own chain token
left `mapSearchGeneral` still comparing the vendor's echoed `chain` against OUR slug. Identical for
`ethereum`/`solana`; different for every chain where the vocabularies diverge — so on ~20 chains
EVERY token row was dropped from a response already paid for. Non-empty, schema-valid, plausible,
missing half its data: the exact class this file's own comments say cost 20 live credits to
diagnose. `EntityLabelsFetchResult` now carries `vendorChain`, and the filter compares against it.

**The rest, by theme.**

- _A predicate and its transport must be one statement._ Both `rpc-solana` and `nansen` gained the
  `servesChain`-shaped single predicate `rpc-evm` already had.
- _An error must not assert what it did not compute._ `CapabilityNotCoveredOnChainError`'s
  `availableCapabilities` is now optional: `undefined` renders nothing, where a caller-supplied `[]`
  used to render "No capability is available on 'ton'" — false, and enough to talk an agent out of
  a call that would have worked.
- _Derived data from immutable inputs belongs to the process, not the request._ `coverage.ts` builds
  its route index once and memoizes both directions; the refusal path takes a bounded
  `servedSlugs(cap, limit)` instead of materialising ~450 slugs to render ten; `list()` matches
  against a precomputed per-row key.
- _The SSRF column is now validated, not merely non-empty._ `https:` only, no userinfo, no IP
  literal — a malformed entry is a load failure, the same class as a duplicate `caip2`. `hostOf`
  throws instead of returning the raw string, so the perimeter has no fail-open path left.
- _The generator reserves the previous snapshot's names BEFORE assigning slugs_ (cycle 5 reserved
  them after the main loop, which protected pass 2 only), and writes its report BEFORE the
  validation gate — a rejected run used to discard the one artifact that explains why.
- _Codegen validates vendor tokens instead of escaping them._ A chain token outside `[a-z0-9._-]`
  is a vendor-drift signal worth a human's attention, not something to quietly encode.

## SEC-1 (2026-07-27) — the daily cap was a damage ceiling; now there is also a brake

The budget guard was denominated in credits per UTC day and nothing bounded the RATE. The
per-provider throttle sustains ~5 paid calls/second ≈ 50 credits/second, so a 2500-credit daily cap
was consumable in under a minute. That was never an over-spend — the ceiling held, the reservation
stayed atomic, the vendor remainder still bound — but a day's entire authorized budget could be
gone before any human noticed, which for an operator is indistinguishable from a leak.

**`usage_window(provider, window_start, credits_used, updated_at)`** — the same additive counter as
`usage`, bucketed by 60 s. A separate table, not a `bucket_width` column: the day counter must keep
summing a day, and two widths in one column would make every existing SELECT ambiguous and force a
migration. Plain `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, same `providers` FK, nothing migrates.

Three things about it are load-bearing, and each was a constraint the issue set out:

- **It is checked and reserved inside the SAME transaction as the daily reservation.** Two
  processes sharing one `cache.sqlite3` would otherwise each pass their own window check against a
  stale read and both spend. `checkAndReserve` takes an optional `velocity: {windowStartMs,
ceiling}` — `BudgetStore` still only ever compares plain numbers and knows nothing about windows,
  minutes or credits, exactly as it knows nothing about `usageAtObserve`.
- **The state is in the ledger, not in memory.** An in-memory limiter is defeated by the same
  process restart that defeated Q-2's L-2.
- **The refusal names the velocity limit.** A rate refusal and a daily refusal call for opposite
  operator responses — wait out the window versus raise a ceiling — so an operator who cannot tell
  them apart fixes the wrong one. The message also states that the daily budget is NOT exhausted.

**The number:** `max(100, ceilingInForce / 20)` per 60 s. Derived rather than absolute for the same
reason `deriveDailyCap` is (owner decision #1: free and Pro, zero code change). The divisor 20 buys
~20 minutes before a day's budget is gone. The floor of 100 is the dearest single call
(`entity.labels` exhaustive) — a limit under one call's cost makes that capability impossible
rather than rate-limited, a worse failure than the one being prevented. Where the floor exceeds
what the daily cap allows, the daily cap binds first; the guards compose and the tighter wins.

**Two properties worth not re-deriving later:**

- The window is **tumbling, not sliding** — up to 2× the allowance across a boundary. Stated in the
  code, the issue and both READMEs rather than implied. A sliding window needs per-call history
  instead of one counter, and 2× does not undermine "buy a human time to notice". If it ever does,
  add a second, wider window; do not rewrite this one.
- **A refund goes back into the window the RESERVATION used**, threaded through `ensureBudget →
reconcile` exactly as `bucket` already was. Refunding into whatever window is current when a
  long call returns would credit a window that never spent — the one way a rate brake can hand a
  runaway loop extra headroom.

**Still open, and deliberately:** Q-3 — a 0-credit tier is unrefusable by ANY credit-denominated
limiter, this one included. Bounding it needs a call-denominated limit, which is a different
mechanism and a separate decision.

## Q-3 + RF-2 (2026-07-27) — a second denominator, and a provenance chain that cannot skip an edit

Two ledger entries closed together because each needed a MECHANISM rather than a patch, and each
had already stated in its own file what that mechanism had to be.

**Q-3 — the credit gate could never refuse a call that costs zero credits.** `entity.labels`' query
tier is priced `{free: 0, pro: 0}`, so `used + 0 > ceiling` is false for the entire life of any
bucket, under any cap, including a deliberately low one. Three review rounds hardened a guard that
structurally could not see this path. The calls are still two real HTTPS round trips against the
operator's account, each carrying up to 200 characters of free text — so every one is a fresh
`args_hash`, a guaranteed cache miss and a new row with an attacker-selectable key.

The fix is a different UNIT, not a tighter number: `usage_window` gained `calls_made` alongside
`credits_used`. Same row, same window, same transaction — a second table would mean a second read
and a second chance for the two to disagree.

Three things worth not re-deriving:

- **The default is FIXED (60/min) while the credit limits are derived.** That asymmetry is the one
  judgement call here. Credits are derived because `free` and `Pro` balances differ by orders of
  magnitude and owner decision #1 requires both to work unchanged. A call is a call on either plan —
  neither vendor rate limits nor cache-row pressure scales with the balance, so there is nothing to
  derive FROM.
- **A call is never refunded.** `recordDelta` passes `calls: 0`. The vendor round trip happened;
  letting reconciliation refund it would let cheap-then-refunded calls walk past the limit that
  exists to bound exactly that traffic.
- **Only two of the issue's three consequences are closed.** Vendor exposure and cache-row growth,
  yes (at 60/min against a 3600s TTL, rows settle at ~3600). _Price drift detectable in advance_ is
  NOT closed and cannot be by any pre-call gate — it stays `reconcile()`'s job. Said in the issue
  rather than quietly counted as fixed.

The column arrived after the table shipped, so `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` does not add it:
`SqliteBudgetStore` checks `PRAGMA table_info` and issues `ALTER TABLE … ADD COLUMN`
(`USAGE_WINDOW_COLUMNS`). Idempotent per open, additive, no backfill — the "mechanical, not a
project" migration shape DB-SCHEMA-CONCEPT §1 requires, and the first time this repo has needed it.

**RF-2 — the golden-test SHA chain had a window in which it could not detect a silent edit.** The
mechanism failed structurally, not carelessly: it required a human to re-run `shasum` and paste the
result, and there is no way to test that a human remembered. Any fix ending in "remember to update
the hash" reproduces the break.

`docs/provenance.json` pins `path → sha256` for the golden test and all nine live-recorded Nansen
fixtures. `scripts/verify-provenance.mjs` recomputes and compares from three sources, and the
choice between them is the whole point:

- `worktree` — run by `packages/core/test/provenance.test.ts` on every `pnpm test`, so drift is
  caught seconds after the edit;
- `index` — run by `.githooks/pre-commit`, i.e. against what is actually being COMMITTED. The
  working tree and the index can differ, and that difference is exactly how the original break
  happened;
- `head` — after-the-fact audit.

**`--update` is in no gate, deliberately.** A check that silently re-recorded the hash it just found
wrong would be worse than no check — every silent edit would become a green build. Re-baselining
puts the edit and the new hash in the SAME diff, which is the property the hand-copied hash was
supposed to have.

**Stated bound on the guarantee:** git hooks are local. `--no-verify`, or a clone that never ran
`git config core.hooksPath .githooks`, bypasses the commit-time half. What survives is the test —
a bypassed commit lands red on the next run anyone does — and the manifest's absence from a diff
that touches a pinned file, which a reviewer can see. The mechanism makes a skipped update LOUD; it
does not make it impossible, and no local-only mechanism can. Files are listed explicitly rather
than globbed, so adding a live-recorded artefact to the pinned set is also a visible decision.

## TASK-007 (2026-07-27) — the free DEX-volume tier, and the size cap that was not one

Third capability on the keyless `defillama` adapter: `dex.volume.history`, from
`GET /overview/dexs/{chain}`. Three of this task's decisions came from a live probe run before the
design, not from the research write-up — each one changed the design.

### `src/adapters/defillama/dex-chains.ts` (GENERATED) + `scripts/gen-defillama-dex-chains.ts`

**`vendors.defillama` is the wrong predicate for this capability, and the gap is 184 chains.** That
registry column was populated from the vendor's TVL catalog (`/v2/chains`) and is non-null for all
458 rows. The vendor's DEX-volume dataset covers 287 chains (`allChains` in a live response), of
which 274 are ours. Reusing the TVL predicate would advertise the capability on 184 chains that have
no such data — TASK-006's H-1 defect class verbatim (coverage widened, transport not).

So `chainSupport(chain, capability)` branches: TVL capabilities keep `vendors.defillama != null`,
`dex.volume.history` requires membership in the generated list. `coversDexVolume()` is the ONE
predicate both the gate and the adapter's own argument validation call — an adapter that answers the
same question in two places eventually answers it two different ways.

The list is a build artifact for the three reasons the chain registry is one (offline gate, CI
determinism, reviewability), generated from committed raw evidence under
`docs/onchain-analytics/raw/`. The generator **refuses** rather than escapes a vendor name outside
`/^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9 ._-]*$/` (space is allowed — `OP Mainnet` is real; quote/backslash/newline
are not, because the value is interpolated into a committed `src/` module), refuses a list under 50
entries, and prints the 13 vendor chains our registry cannot map rather than hiding them.

### `src/adapters/defillama/index.ts` — `fetch`/`normalize` for `dex.volume.history`

- **URL.** `excludeTotalDataChartBreakdown=true` always (the breakdown is the 11.2× difference —
  18 MB vs 1.6 MB globally — and nothing reads it); `excludeTotalDataChart=true` additionally when
  `includeSeries: false`. No headers of any kind: the endpoint is keyless and must stay provably so.
- **Document cache per adapter INSTANCE** (never a module singleton). The vendor has no windowing
  parameter, so 30/90/365 days of one chain is one document — but `days` is part of `argsHash`, so
  the engine's own cache sees three unrelated keys and would download the same 250 KB three times.
  Keyed by `(vendorName, includeSeries)` because `includeSeries` changes the REQUEST; stores the
  PROMISE so concurrent callers share one flight; evicts on rejection so a blip is not remembered
  for an hour. `fetchedAt` is the DOCUMENT's timestamp — two TTL windows in series do not compose
  into one (vdd-multi cycle 6, M-1).
- **`normalize()` verifies the response's `chain` echo first.** Forced by measured behaviour: the
  path is name-tolerant (`op-mainnet`/`optimism`/`OP Mainnet` → one document), an unknown chain
  answers **HTTP 500** (not 404), and a chain outside the active set answers **HTTP 200 with zeros
  and a narrower key set**. Without the check, "served a different chain" and "this chain has no
  volume" are the same observation — and the wrong document gets cached under our slug.
- **Units and refusals.** Vendor `date` is unix SECONDS → `ts = date*1000`, epoch-ms UTC
  (DB-SCHEMA §1.2). An implausible timestamp drops its point (a decoding question about one point);
  a non-finite or negative volume throws BEFORE the cache write (the document is not what we think
  it is). Aggregates are `number | null` — never `0` for a key the vendor omitted, which would be a
  fabricated measurement.
- **`gapDays` counts, it never stitches.** An interpolated point is a number nobody measured, and
  dropping the count makes an incomplete answer look complete.
- **The truncation flag is a granularity-drift guard, and the obvious version of it is dead code.**
  The window is `args.days` long and `days` is bounded, so a separate "max points" constant could
  never fire — a daily series in an N-day window has at most N points by construction. What CAN
  exceed it is the vendor switching to sub-daily points or emitting duplicates under an unchanged
  endpoint; that is what the cap catches, and `truncated` therefore keeps meaning "you did not get
  what you asked for" instead of being set by ordinary windowing.
- **No vendor free text reaches the result.** The document carries 151 protocol cards with
  `name`/`category`/`methodology`/`logo` — third-party-editable strings. None is read; `chain`/`name`
  come from our registry and `source` is a literal. A test asserts a planted injection string cannot
  appear in the serialized output.

### `src/net/safe-fetch.ts` — the response-size cap became real (closes R-47 item 1)

The cap read the advertised `Content-Length` and returned early when the header was absent. That is
the common case, not the exotic one: **`api.llama.fi` sends no `Content-Length` on any response**
(HTTP/2 + Cloudflare, measured 2026-07-27), so the cap was inert on a host this package already
called on two capabilities and was about to call on a third. `capResponseStream()` now counts bytes
off `response.body` when no header is present and **cancels the upstream reader** on overflow —
stopping the transfer, not merely declining to look at it. Status/statusText/headers are carried
over verbatim so every existing caller behaves identically, and a response that DOES advertise a
length still takes the cheaper header path untouched.

The error surfaces where the body is READ, inside the calling adapter's `fetch()`. That placement is
correct rather than incidental: `CapabilityRegistry` treats a `fetch()` throw as "try the next
adapter" and deliberately does NOT negative-cache it — an oversized response is a transport
condition, not the deterministic `normalize()` verdict negative caching exists for.

### `src/providers.config.ts` — `defillama` rate limit raised to `{capacity: 10, refillPerSec: 5}`

The old `{5, 1}` was OUR brake, not the vendor's: the vendor publishes no numeric limit, and a live
cache-busted probe took 40 concurrent origin requests with 40/40 HTTP 200 and zero 429s. At 1
token/s a ten-chain sweep spent ~5 s asleep in our own limiter and a wider one crossed
`MAX_WAIT_MS = 30_000` and began throwing. One bucket serves all three defillama capabilities, so
raising it can only speed the two existing paths up.

### WI-26 — `test/helpers/isolated-throttle.ts` (new), `test/throttle-seam.test.ts` (new)

Tests constructing an adapter without a `throttle` fell back to the module-level production
singleton, whose bucket map is created once per process; vitest isolates per FILE, so every test in
a file shared one narrow bucket and the later ones slept on real timers. Measured: **46.8 s of the
core suite's 48.5 s cumulative test time**, across five files (`blockscout.transport` 23.5 s,
`defillama-dex-volume` 12.8 s, `blockscout-fallback` 8.0 s, `registry.fallback` 2.0 s,
`blockscout-disabled` 0.5 s). The record
named one of the four.

Six of the twelve adapters had **no seam at all** — `coingecko`, `defillama`, `dexscreener`,
`platform-explorer`, `rpc-evm`, `rpc-solana` imported the singleton directly, so a test could not
inject a limiter even if it wanted to. All six now expose `throttle?: Throttle` and resolve
`deps.throttle ?? productionThrottle`, matching `blockscout`/`blockchain-info`/`nansen`.

- **`test/helpers/isolated-throttle.ts`** — `createThrottle` on a VIRTUAL clock: `wait()` advances
  the clock instead of the wall, so refill/deficit/weight arithmetic is real for sequentially
  awaited calls and nothing sleeps. It deliberately **cannot** reproduce saturation or the
  `MAX_WAIT_MS` fairness cap (its `wait` runs inside the synchronous prefix, so deficits never
  accumulate); that behaviour is owned by `test/rate-limit.test.ts`, which drives its own clock.
- **`test/throttle-seam.test.ts`** — the structural gate: every adapter that IMPORTS the limiter
  must declare the seam, resolve it, and name `productionThrottle` nowhere but its import and that
  resolution. Membership is keyed on the import (a rename cannot drop an adapter from the set) and
  the population is an exact list, not a floor. Both refinements came from the adversarial cycle,
  which defeated the first version twice: by renaming the local binding, and by keeping the seam
  while sending one of `defillama`'s three call sites straight to the singleton.
- **Residual, stated:** 14 of the 26 core test files that construct a throttling adapter still take
  the singleton (12 inject one). None measurably waits today (all under 50 ms) and nothing here would notice if one
  grew a loop.

## T-012 task 012-1 (2026-08-04) — `CapabilityRoute.chains` deleted (OQ-C)

Two mechanisms had narrowed chain coverage for a route since TASK-006: `adapter.chainSupport()`
(the coverage matrix + the per-adapter skip in `resolve()`) and an optional `chains?: Chain[]`
literal still declared on `CapabilityRoute` and still read by a `resolve()` filter
(`!candidate.chains || candidate.chains.includes(chain)`) — TASK-006 had already made the literal
redundant for every real route but never removed it. An audit of all 21 routes in
`providers.config.ts` (task 012-1) found **zero** setting it. Removed:

- `CapabilityRoute.chains?: Chain[]` (`src/adapters/types.ts`) — the interface docstring now states
  chain coverage is a property of the `(adapter, capability)` pair, not of the route.
- The route-level filter in `CapabilityRegistry.resolve()` (`src/adapters/registry.ts`) —
  `matching` now filters on `capability` alone; the per-adapter `chainSupport()` skip (step 3,
  unchanged) is the only remaining mechanism.
- `providers.config.ts`'s docstring, which had claimed the field "remains part of `CapabilityRoute`
  and is still honoured if set" — corrected to the historical past tense.

**Falsifying order, not a red phase (this is a deletion, not new behaviour):** the risk named in the
task file was a "proof that proves nothing" — `test/registry.test.ts`'s route-selection test built
fakes with NO `chainSupport`, so removing the filter without fixing the fakes would fail at RUNTIME
for the right reason, and "fixing" it by rewriting the assertions would silently drop the only test
guarding chain-based route selection. Order actually followed: (1) give the fakes a real
`chainSupport` distinguishing `rpc-evm`/`rpc-solana`, filter still in code, still-agreeing mechanisms
→ green; (2) remove the route's `chains:` literal from THAT test only, filter still in code → still
green — this is the falsifying step, proving `chainSupport()` alone already decided the outcome;
(3) delete the field and the filter; (4) remove the remaining 12 `chains:` literals across 8 other
test files (`CapabilityDescriptor.chains` in the same files, e.g. `capabilities: () => [{ id, chains
}]`, is a DIFFERENT field and was left untouched).

**One consequence the 13-literal audit did not name:** `test/coverage.test.ts` had a runtime
regression test from TASK-006 ("no route carries a `chains` literal any more (R-54)") that read
`route.chains !== undefined` on the real `providers.config.ts` routes — a property access, not a
literal `chains:` write, so it wasn't in the audit's grep. Deleting the field turned that into a
compile error. Rewritten as a compile-time trap instead of a weaker runtime check: an object literal
typed `CapabilityRoute` with a `chains:` key under `@ts-expect-error` — if the field is ever
reintroduced, the directive becomes "unused" and `tsc` fails the build, which is a stronger guarantee
than any route table could prove at runtime. Verified both directions by hand: temporarily
re-adding the field made `tsc --noEmit` fail with `TS2578: Unused '@ts-expect-error' directive`;
temporarily short-circuiting the `chainSupport()` skip in `resolve()` made the rewritten
route-selection test fail with `expected 'rpc-evm' to be 'rpc-solana'` — both reverted after
confirming.

## T-012 task 012-2 (2026-08-04) — `tier` and `trust` declared on all 12 registrations (ADR-002 D8 + D9 slice)

`AdapterRegistration` (`src/adapters/types.ts`) gains **two required fields**, and all twelve
entries in `src/providers.config.ts` carry them. Both value sets are exported as named unions,
`AdapterTier = 'free' | 'paid'` and `AdapterTrust = 'authoritative' | 'derived' | 'community'`.

- **`tier`** — the ONE classification of paidness, introduced to replace four competing ones
  (`PAID_PROVIDER_IDS` in `cache/sqlite-store.ts`, the `providers.kind` column, `BudgetMeta.provider`'s
  hand-picked literal type, and `costOf()` read as a de-facto tier signal). A static property of the
  **vendor relationship**, deliberately never derived from `costOf()`, which varies with the
  arguments and the live account plan.
- **`trust`** — declare-only in T-012 (R-155). The single reader in the whole codebase is
  `assertValidAdapterRegistrations()`; merge segmentation, community marking and the per-ROW
  `source → trust` dictionary are **T-016**. Audited after the change: `grep -rn '\.trust\b'` over
  both packages' `src` returns **nothing** — the only access is the validator's own
  `registration[field]` over `['tier','trust']`.

**Assignments (owner decision OD-5, 2026-08-03, closing OQ-T012-2/OQ-T012-3).** `paid` — `dune`,
`nansen`. `community` — `blockscout` (ADR-002 D9's own worked example) and `pg-history`. `derived` —
**nobody**: it describes individual ROWS (`source='derived'`), not an adapter.
`platform-explorer` is `authoritative` because D9's scale asks about the **editability of the
content**, not the officialness of the operator — and it is the source D6 turns merging on first.
`pg-history` is `community` as a deliberate **placeholder** whose replacement is assigned to T-016;
its inline comment says so explicitly, because the next reader would otherwise "fix" it.

**`assertValidAdapterRegistrations(registrations)` lives in `adapters/types.ts`, not on
`CapabilityRegistry`** — `AdapterRegistration` never reaches the registry at all; it goes to
`SqliteCacheStore`/`SqliteBudgetStore`'s `bootstrapProviders()` and nowhere else, so a check hosted
by the registry would sit on a path the data does not travel. The array is a **parameter, never a
module import**: a validator that reads its own subject can only ever be exercised against the one
real complete input. `mcp-server/src/index.ts` calls it at **module scope**, so a bad registration
kills process start rather than the first request that routes through it.

**Red phase, and what each new test would miss without its mechanism** (all three breaks made and
reverted, `test/adapter-registrations.test.ts`):

- Phase 1 (empty validator body) was red on 3 core cases + TC-INT-01, all with "expected undefined
  to be an instance of Error". The compile-time half could not be red: the fields are required from
  the moment they are declared, which is why the 12 assignments had to land in phase 1 too.
- **TC-UNIT-03** — removing `'${id}'` from the message (keeping the throw) turned all three
  validator cases red: `expected 'adapter registration does not declare…' to contain 'probe-adapter'`.
  The test asserts the id SUBSTRING, not the fact of a throw.
- **TC-UNIT-03, second defect — caught in review, not by me.** The first version built the broken
  entry as `{ ...PROBE }` without overriding `id`, so the offender and the leading entry shared the
  id `probe-adapter`, while its own comment claimed "a validator that names the first or the last
  entry regardless cannot pass this". It could: a mutant resolving
  `const id = String(registrations[0]?.id ?? 'x')` — always the first entry, never the failing one —
  passed the file **16/16**. The three ids are now distinct (`probe-leading`, the offender,
  `probe-trailing`) and the negative assertions carry the weight: the message must NOT name either
  neighbour. Re-run against both mutants (`registrations[0]` and `registrations[length-1]`): 3 red
  each, e.g. `expected 'adapter registration \'probe-leading\…' to contain 'probe-undeclared'`.
  The offender ids also deliberately avoid the substrings `tier`/`trust`
  (`probe-undeclared`/`probe-unpriced`), or `expect(message).toContain("'trust'")` would be
  satisfied by the id alone — the same trick one level down.
- **TC-UNIT-06** — the structural gate. Adding
  `const tier = costOf(deps.accountState, cap, args) > 0 ? 'paid' : 'free'` to
  `adapters/nansen/budget-gate.ts` produced a named failure quoting the file and the statement.
  It is structural because the value-level phrasing is **unfalsifiable**: `tier` is a required
  literal and no code path runs from `costOf()` to it, so a value assertion passes under every
  implementation including the wrong one. The gate ships four of its own controls — it must detect
  the direct, the **aliased** (`costOf as price`) and the **two-step** (`const c = costOf(); const
isPaid = c > 0`) forms, and must NOT flag a file that merely prices with `costOf`, or it would
  degenerate into "no file may import `costOf`", which is not the rule. Comments and string
  contents are blanked by a single scanner rather than a regex chain, because
  `.replace(/\/\/.*$/)` blanks the tail of any line holding a `'https://…'` literal — a blind line
  is a false negative, which is the failure a gate cannot afford.
- **TC-INT-01** — lives in `packages/mcp-server/test/`; the corruption is injected via `vi.mock`,
  never written into `providers.config.ts`. It carries **two non-interchangeable assertions**, and
  the first write-up of this bullet misattributed them (also caught in review). Moving the call
  below `createBudgetStore()` INSIDE `main()` kills assertion 1 — `expected undefined to be an
instance of Error` — because `main().catch(… process.exit(1))` swallows the throw and the import
  resolves; the ORDER assertion never even executes. The ORDER assertion dies on a different
  mutant: a store constructed at MODULE SCOPE ahead of the validator, where the import still
  rejects and still names the id and only the spy log is non-empty
  (`expected [ 'createBudgetStore' ] to deeply equal []`). Both mutants were run; each kills only
  its own assertion. See that file's docstring, which now states the mapping.

**`TC-UNIT-05` enumerates, it does not count.** Both assignment maps are compared whole
(`toEqual`), so a permutation, a missing id or a thirteenth registration all fail. A count would
have passed on a swap — and after OD-5 gave `community` a second holder, "exactly one `community`"
would additionally have been wrong.

**Stale statements elsewhere in this file, named rather than silently rewritten.** The `kind`
paragraph above (`SqliteCacheStore` bootstrap) says "`AdapterRegistration` itself carries no such
field". As of this task it does — that is what `tier` is. The same claim sits in the source at
`src/cache/sqlite-store.ts:35-42`, which task **012-3** deletes along with `PAID_PROVIDER_IDS` when
the bootstrap starts writing `registration.tier`; both were left untouched here deliberately, so
the next pass does not have to guess whether they were missed or reserved. **Both were corrected in
012-3 — the section below.**

## T-012 task 012-3 (2026-08-04) — the two `providers.kind` writers became readers of `tier`

Two of the four competing classifications of paidness lived in `src/cache/`; both are gone.

- **`sqlite-store.ts`** — `PAID_PROVIDER_IDS = new Set(['dune','nansen'])` and the whole comment
  block above it are deleted (not just the line): the comment's justification — "`AdapterRegistration`
  itself carries no such field" — was made false by 012-2, and a comment outliving its reason
  describes behaviour the code no longer has. `bootstrapProviders` binds `registration.tier` into
  `kind`. The same method's docstring said "bootstrap from ALL **9** `adapterRegistrations`"; it is
  **12** (the fourth stale count of this task family, fixed in the commit that rewrites the method
  body it describes — the other three were 012-2's).
- **`budget-store.ts`** — the bootstrap wrote the literal `kind: 'unknown'` under
  `ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING` and now writes `registration.tier` under
  `ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE SET kind = excluded.kind`, the same form its `SqliteCacheStore`
  sibling already used. **Both halves of the old rule had a stated reason and the reason is recorded
  as SUPERSEDED in the class docstring rather than deleted:** `'unknown'` avoided replicating a
  hardcoded id list into a second file, and `DO NOTHING` kept a budget store from clobbering a row a
  cache store had already classified correctly. Once both writers derive one value from one field,
  there is nothing to clobber — and `DO NOTHING` would instead pin every pre-012-3 file's twelve
  rows at `'unknown'` forever. A diff that removed the sentence would read as a protection being
  dropped, which is the one thing it must not be mistaken for.
- **`test/budget-velocity.test.ts`** — the three registration fixtures (`:33`, `:137`, `:394`) were
  `[{ id, hosts, rateLimit: { rps: 1 } }] as never`. The cast hid a `rateLimit` shape that never
  existed AND 012-2's new required fields, so the breakage skipped the build and would have arrived
  at runtime here (`providers.kind` is `TEXT NOT NULL`; better-sqlite3 refuses to bind `undefined`).
  Replaced by one real `AdapterRegistration` constant with `nansen`'s true ranks; **`as never`
  removed, not filled in**. No `?? 'free'` anywhere in either bootstrap: a missing `tier` must be a
  loud fixture error, never an invented value (R-150(c)).

**`test/tier-single-source.test.ts` (new)** — two structural gates plus the writer unit cases.

- **TC-GATE-01** scans both packages' `src` for a paidness decision keyed on a provider ID, via
  three detectors (a declaration of string-literal ids whose name says what it is; `.has(id)`/
  `.includes(id)`; `id === 'literal'`), each requiring the paidness vocabulary nearby. It reads
  CODE (comments and string CONTENTS blanked, quotes kept), which is what lets it stay silent on
  the five legitimate `'nansen'` literals — three route `adapterIds`, the registration's own `id`,
  and `mcp-server`'s adapter-map key. **The first draft of this gate banned the literal and would
  have fired on all five.** It deliberately does NOT flag `r.tier === 'paid'`: the left operand's
  last segment decides, and that one is the single-source READ.
- **TC-GATE-02** — no `.trust` read in either `src` outside `adapters/types.ts`. Today the offender
  set is empty even inside the exemption, because the validator reads `registration[field]`; the
  gate's claim is that a SECOND reader cannot appear unnoticed, and its controls prove the detector
  fires on a real read. Scan roots are `src` only — the sibling `nonEmpty` gate also scans `test`,
  and copying that would fail on 012-2's own deliberate `.trust` test reads.
- **TC-UNIT-01/02/02a/03** feed both stores a registration table that **contradicts the real world**
  (`nansen` declared `free`, `coingecko` declared `paid`). That inversion is the entire mechanism:
  fed the real twelve, the same assertions pass under an implementation carrying its own id list,
  because list and table agree today. Verified by mutation — reinstating a `PAID` set in
  `sqlite-store.ts` failed TC-GATE-01 **and** TC-UNIT-01/03, while the "real twelve" case stayed
  green exactly as predicted.
- **Both gates DECLARE their recall limit** (review round 1). The first version's docstring said
  "every place … three forms"; review measured three forms walking past it. Each gate now carries a
  reached/not-reached table, every reached form has a positive control, and the **not-reached rows
  are asserted too** — so a future widening that closes one fails the limit test and forces the
  table to be updated with it. Closed in this round: `switch (providerId) { case 'nansen': … }`,
  `['dune','nansen'].find((x) => x === id)`, `TABLE[providerId]`, and `.indexOf(id)` (which the
  review's own probe listed as already covered — measuring it said otherwise). Declared as out of
  reach: a decision split across statements or a function boundary, a lookup table's declaration
  with bare-identifier keys, vocabulary outside `paid`/`tier`/`kind`, and an id list arriving at
  runtime.
- **`test/adapter-registrations.test.ts` — a 012-2 file, edited in 012-3 for this one fix.** Its
  `costOf` gate had the same undeclared-recall defect and missed the **default** and **namespace**
  import forms (`import costOf from './cost-of.js'`, `import * as prices from …` → `prices.costOf(…)`),
  neither of which contains the name `costOf` in the import statement, so no binding was discovered
  and the file scanned as if it never priced anything. Both are now bound (matched on the RAW source,
  because a module specifier is string content that `blankNonCode` erases), each with a positive
  control, plus a declared-limits test. **Nothing else in that file was reopened** — the fix travels
  with the finding that produced it, per PLAN §0.4's ownership rule.
- **TC-UNIT-02a** seeds `('nansen','unknown')` on a file whose tables are created by running
  `CACHE_DDL` directly — not by constructing a store, which would bootstrap the rows before the
  seed could exist. Verified by mutation: restoring `DO NOTHING` failed it with
  `expected { coingecko: 'unknown', … } to strictly equal { coingecko: 'paid', … }`. On a fresh
  database that same test passes under either conflict clause, which is why the seed is mandatory.

## T-012 task 012-4 (2026-08-04) — `src/capability-manifest.ts`: 20 rows, validated at construction

**New: `src/capability-manifest.ts`** — the ADR-002 **D3** manifest, one committed literal covering
all 20 routed capabilities (21 routes; `wallet.balances.native` is routed twice). Tier-1
configuration in the commit (D1): checked once when `CapabilityRegistry` is constructed, held in
process memory, never in the cache DB or Postgres. It carries `shape`, `ttlSeconds`, `deadlineMs`
and — only where a route reaches a `tier: 'paid'` adapter — `paidLegMs`. `shareable` is declared and
unused (first reader is ADR-003 D5, T-014).

- **The type is a two-branch discriminated union, and what that buys today is stated exactly.** Both
  branches currently differ ONLY by the `shape` literal, so a merge field added to the shared BASE
  would be legal on `point` too: the union does not make the restriction **effective**, it makes it
  **expressible**. T-013 adds the field to the `set | series` branch alone and gets the compile error
  for free. Rejected (task F-5): `mergeKey?: never` on the `point` branch today — that pre-picks a
  NAME T-013 has not chosen, and a guard with no input on which it fails is barred here as everywhere
  else in this task.
- **`ttlSeconds` are a MOVE, not an edit.** All 20 are carried byte-for-byte out of `cache/ttl.ts`'s
  `TTL_SECONDS`, rationales included. `ttlFor()` becomes a reader of this table in **012-5**; until
  then `cache/ttl.ts` is still the live path and this is its copy — hence TC-UNIT-05 pins every value
  against a list transcribed BEFORE the move (a live comparison would be satisfied by two tables
  changed together).
- **`shape` for the 12 capabilities ADR-002 D3 had not classified was decided by READING
  `normalize()`**, not by carrying the plan's hypotheses over. 10 hypotheses confirmed, 2 the plan
  left open (`smart-money.flows`, `token.risk`) resolved to `point`; **zero contradicted**. The rule
  the audit had to make explicit: the discriminator is the SUBSTANCE of the payload, not whether the
  top-level return is an array — D3's own eight settle it, since `wallet.balances.native` is `set`
  while `rpc-evm.normalize()` returns a single `Wallet` whose whole content is `balances[]`. Two
  rows would have been classified wrongly by an "is it an array?" reading: `dex.volume.history`
  (one `DexVolumeResult` object → `series`, its `series[]` explicitly `ts`-sorted) and `pool.info`
  (a `Pool[]` despite the singular name — `dexscreener.normalize` ignores `_cap` and serves both its
  capabilities identically).
- **Every `deadlineMs`/`paidLegMs` carries a two-number record (R-149):** the measured envelope AND
  the applied value, which is which, and what the ceiling cuts. Four envelopes are derived once at
  the top of the file and cited by name — `E-HTTP15 = 90_000` (30_000 limiter + 4 hops × 15_000),
  `E-HTTP5 = 50_000`, `E-PG`, `E-DASH = 0`. `paidLegMs` is the worst case over ARGUMENTS
  (`entity.labels` issues 2 OR 3 sub-calls under one reservation) and is the one place where applied
  EQUALS measured: OD-2/OD-3 bound the cancellable part only, and after `checkAndReserve()`
  cancelling would mean paying without receiving.
- **One row is an OVERRIDE of an approved architecture number, and says so.**
  `privacy.shielded_pool` + the four `platform.*` get **15_000**, not the `system-architecture.md`
  table's ~30_000: their second adapter `dash-platform` has an unconditionally false `isAvailable()`
  and performs zero attempts, so the route is single-LIVE-adapter. Reason and revert condition (a
  live gRPC transport makes E-DASH non-zero and the number returns to the two-adapter tier) sit in
  the code beside the number. The architecture row is addressed **by its capability list, never by
  line number**: the visually identical ~30_000 row directly beneath it (the two `*.history`
  capabilities) is CORRECT and untouched. Hand-off order is mandatory — override marked here (012-4)
  → document edited **as an override** in 012-5 → decision closed in `open-questions.md` in 012-10.
- **Two derivations do NOT reuse the HTTP template, because the mechanics differ.** `dash-platform`
  adds zero and the record says the number grows the day a live gRPC transport lands. `pg-history`
  speaks the Postgres wire protocol: no redirect hops, and — **measured 2026-08-04, contradicting
  PLAN §0.2a** — its `fetch()` never awaits `throttle()` at all, so `MAX_WAIT_MS` never enters its
  envelope despite the registration declaring `{capacity: 2, refillPerSec: 0.2}`. What bounds it is
  `connectionTimeoutMillis = 10_000` (`pg/read-client.ts`'s `DEFAULT_CONNECTION_TIMEOUT_MS`) plus a
  query with no `statement_timeout` anywhere — an unbounded tail.
  **CORRECTED by adversarial cycle 2, F-5:** this bullet used to end "…whose only in-process bound
  is the 30_000 ceiling this task applies". There is no such bound. `pg-history` is handed no
  deadline (`fetch(cap, args)`) and neither is `platform-explorer`, so **nothing** in-process
  cancels that tail — the ceiling is declared and not enforced on either `*.history` capability.
  Measured 2026-08-05: 2 of 12 adapters read `deadlineAtMs` (`blockscout`, `nansen`), so 4 of 20
  capabilities are actually bounded by their manifest row (`token.holders`, `entity.labels`,
  `smart-money.flows`, `token.risk`). Sanctioned by R-140e and tracked as **WI-37**; the ENFORCEMENT
  section of `capability-manifest.ts` carries the measurement, and each row marks itself ENFORCED or
  DECLARED.
  **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-05 by WI-34/WI-35/WI-37** (see the closing section of this file): the pg tail
  is bounded (20 000 ms in-process, 5 000 ms `statement_timeout`), the declared limiter is applied,
  E-PG is **50 000** rather than "≥10 000 unbounded", and 10 of 12 adapters read the deadline, so
  **20 of 20** capabilities are bounded. The paragraph above is kept because it is the state the
  012-4 numbers were derived in.

**`src/adapters/registry.ts`** — fifth constructor parameter
`manifests = capabilityManifests`, the same seam `chains` already had one parameter to the left
(production default is the real table, tests inject their own). **Validation step 1 runs in the
constructor body, not lazily in `resolve()`:** a missing row throws
`MissingCapabilityManifestError`, so a misconfigured table stops process start (the discipline
`assertValidAdapterRegistrations()` established in 012-2) instead of surfacing months later on the
first call of one capability. The docstring fixes the ORDER of validation before there is a second
step to order — **step 1 manifest, step 2 policy class (012-6)** — so each requirement's negative
test can break exactly one thing.

**`test/coverage.test.ts` — exactly two call sites, as the plan measured.** `:86` (inside
`registryWith`) and `:171` (a direct construction the helper does not cover, because it injects a
spying cache), both fed a three-row map for `ROUTES`' synthetic `legacy.thing`. `ghost` (`:255`) and
`x` (`:127`/`:139`) are **untouched and must not be cited as touched**: the first is an argument to
`createCoverage({routes})`, the second a string handed to `CapabilityNotCoveredOnChainError`; neither
passes through registry validation.

**`test/capability-manifest.test.ts` (new)** — TC-UNIT-01…08 + TC-INT-01…03, 62 cases.

- **Two of them read the module's SOURCE** (precedent: `ttl-coverage.test.ts` reading its own
  table), and **both declare their recall limit** in the form 012-3 established — a
  reached/not-reached table, a positive control per reached form, and executable assertions that the
  not-reached rows really are out of reach.
- **TC-UNIT-07 guards the DECLARATION, not the merge rule.** It fails when the union collapses,
  grows a third branch, loses the shared base, or merges the discriminators. Declared out of reach
  and asserted: a merge field added to `CapabilityManifestBase` (the very thing the union does not
  prevent today), `capabilityManifests` re-typed to some other type, and `CapabilityManifestBase`
  redeclared as something unrelated. A test claiming "merging cannot be declared on `point`" would
  be FALSE here and is not written.
- **TC-UNIT-08 guards the PRESENCE of two numbers, not the arithmetic.** It requires a
  `measured envelope:` number and an `applied:` number in the comment block adjacent to each of the
  23 numeric fields, with `applied:` equal to the literal. Measured escapes, asserted as limits: a
  fabricated measured number, a measured number copied from another capability's row,
  measured == applied (required to stay legal — `paidLegMs` has them equal by design), and a field
  whose value is a named constant rather than a numeric literal. Verifying a number against its
  documented derivation is the extended WI-28 gate in **012-5**.
- **Every gate was verified by mutation**: dropping the `token.holders` row
  (`expected [ 'token.price', …(18) ] to include 'token.holders'`), adding an orphan row
  (`expected [ 'legacy.orphan' ] to strictly equal []`), moving one TTL 1800→1801
  (`- "token.risk": 1800, + "token.risk": 1801`), collapsing the union
  (`expected [ Array(1) ] to have a length of 2 but got 1`), reducing one derivation record to a
  single number (``no `measured envelope:` number in the adjacent record``), and making the manifest
  check lazy (`expected function to throw an error, but it didn't`).

## T-012 task 012-5 (2026-08-04) — `ttlFor()` is a manifest reader; `TTL_SECONDS` is gone

**`src/cache/ttl.ts`** — from 108 lines to 62. The `TTL_SECONDS` table is **deleted** (its 20 values
and every rationale moved to `capability-manifest.ts` in 012-4, byte for byte); `ttlFor()` now
returns `capabilityManifests[capability]?.ttlSeconds ?? DEFAULT_TTL_SECONDS`. **No TTL value
changed** — `ttl-coverage.test.ts`'s TC-UNIT-01 pins all 20 against a list written out as literals,
because a list computed from the manifest would only restate the implementation.

- **The contract was the deliverable.** Same name, same signature, same export path
  (`src/index.ts:91`), so `mcp-server/test/readme-tool-table.test.ts` — the reason `ttlFor` is
  exported at all — needed no edit to its `ttlFor` import or calls (AC-5; the import line is
  byte-identical in the diff, and the manifest arrives through a second import statement).
- **`DEFAULT_TTL_SECONDS = 300` stays, and its docstring now says what makes it unreachable.** It
  used to say the constant "should not be hit", which is a hope — and it was disappointed three
  times (three M2 paid capabilities, then `dex.volume.history`). What closes it is not this file:
  it is `CapabilityRegistry`'s construction-time validation step 1 (012-4), which refuses to build a
  registry for a route with no manifest row. The constant's remaining job — an unrouted capability
  string — is named, so the next reader does not delete it as dead.
- **`NEGATIVE_TTL_SECONDS = 60` did not move**, and the reason is now written beside it: it is a
  property of the FAILURE path, not of any capability, so a per-capability table is the wrong home.

**`test/ttl-coverage.test.ts` — retargeted, 23 cases → 50.** It reads
`src/capability-manifest.ts` for `'<id>': { … ttlSeconds: <n> … }` records instead of `cache/ttl.ts`
for `'<id>': <n>,` rows. Both directions are kept (every routed capability has a written-out row; no
orphan rows), and the vacuity guard stays the file's first test.

- **The phase order is the evidence, and it is not cosmetic.** Retargeting first would have passed on
  the first run — the manifest was already complete — proving nothing. `TTL_SECONDS` was deleted
  FIRST, and the old regex then matched zero rows: `expected 0 to be greater than 10`, 21 of 23 cases
  red. That run is what makes the guard a mechanism rather than decoration, and it is recorded in the
  file's docstring.
- **The row scan carries the reached / NOT-reached table 012-3 established**, with controls. Two of
  its declared limits were **wrong when first written and were corrected by measuring them**: a
  `// '<id>': { … }` line is _not_ counted (the comment marker defeats the `^\s+'` anchor — only a
  block-comment line that itself begins with the key is), and `/negative/i` over the manifest source
  matches the prose "the negative type-test that T-012 cannot write", so TC-UNIT-03 checks manifest
  KEYS plus the identifier `negativeTtl` instead of a word.
- **TC-UNIT-02 asserts `ttlFor('capability.which.nothing.routes') === 300`** with the number written
  out: `DEFAULT_TTL_SECONDS` is not exported, and "a test wants it" is not the justification
  `index.ts` accepts for widening this module's public surface.

## T-012 task 012-6 (2026-08-04) — the route policy is a descriptor; `isSatisfying` is gone

**New: `src/adapters/policy.ts`** — `PolicyDescriptor` (`{ kind: 'any' }` |
`{ kind: 'someElementHasAny'; fields: string[] }`) plus `policyClasses`, the dictionary those names
resolve against (ADR-002 **D2**, closes OQ-4). `CapabilityRoute.isSatisfying` — a literal function —
is **deleted**; the one production carrier (`entity.labels`) now declares
`policy: { kind: 'someElementHasAny', fields: ['name','tags','labels'] }`. The other 20 routes carry
nothing, and **absence IS `{ kind: 'any' }`** (TC-UNIT-04 asserts the two are indistinguishable at
the registry, rather than restating it in prose).

- **Two classes; no third.** The first candidate ("is what I have COLLECTED enough") is a different
  question and arrives with D5/T-013's merge rule. A class with no consumer is a guess about the
  consumer, so it is not written.
- **`someElementHasAny` is not named after emptiness, and the ban is on IDENTIFIERS only.** A literal
  "the array is not empty" reading reproduces H-1 word for word: Blockscout answers with an array of
  ONE contentless entry, and a length-based predicate would accept it and shadow the paid source for
  3600 s. The explanation must survive in PROSE (R-134c) — it lives in the docstrings of `policy.ts`
  and `types.ts` — so the gate reads two views and bans the name in one of them (below).
- **Content vs presence** is the whole semantic: a non-empty string or a non-empty array. `['']`
  counts (a vendor that returned a tag made a statement); `''` does not.
- **The equivalence with the retired literal is claimed ON THE TASK-008 FIXTURE SET, never in
  general** (TC-UNIT-03, 8 fixtures, 2 satisfying / 6 not, zero disagreements). Exactly one input
  diverges **deliberately**: the literal tested `typeof name === 'string'`, so `name: ''` satisfied
  it — the very contentless record H-1 is about. `EntityLabelSchema.name` is
  `z.string().max(256).optional()` with no `.min(1)`, so that input is representable, and TC-UNIT-03a
  parses it through the schema before pinning the NEW verdict as correct. The tightening is recorded,
  not discovered later by someone loosening the class to make a test pass.

**`src/adapters/registry.ts`** — **validation step 2**, in the constructor, strictly after step 1
(manifest, 012-4): an unresolvable `policy.kind` throws `UnregisteredPolicyClassError` naming **both**
the capability and the kind. The resolved predicate is cached **per route object**, never per
capability — a capability-keyed map would rebuild M-2 (`wallet.balances.native` already ships as two
routes for one capability). `satisfies()` is behaviourally untouched: same fail-open, same single
stderr line, same verdict; only what it calls changed.

- **The two steps are two SEPARATE loops.** With one loop the order would hold per ROUTE, so which
  error a doubly-broken table reports would depend on row position. TC-INT-02 carries a second case
  for exactly this, and the merged-loop mutation kills that case and no other.

**`test/policy.test.ts` (new)** — TC-UNIT-01…04, TC-GATE-01, TC-INT-01/02/03/05: 42 cases.

- **TC-GATE-01 scans 188 files under both packages' `src` AND `test`, in two views**: a CODE view
  (comments and string contents blanked) for identifiers, and a STRING view for test titles and
  quoted keys. Reached, each with a control: export name, alias, dictionary key, type name,
  `NON_EMPTY`/casing variants, a test title, a quoted key. **NOT reached, asserted as limits:** the
  name in a comment (required — it is the R-134c explanation), a name assembled at runtime, synonyms
  (`notEmpty` normalizes to `notempty`), and anything outside the declared roots —
  `packages/mcp-server/eval/checks.mjs` holds 18 occurrences of an unrelated eval helper by that
  name, and the test PROVES it would be flagged if scanned, so the boundary is measured rather than
  assumed.
- **No file is exempt, including this gate's own.** The precedent (`tier-single-source.test.ts`
  exempts `types.ts` from its `.trust` gate) was available and rejected: the controls assemble the
  banned name from fragments instead, which costs two `+` signs and removes the hole from the one
  file most likely to acquire an alias. It also makes the "assembled at runtime" limit
  self-demonstrating.
- **One scanner behaviour is ADDED, not inherited:** a quote opens a string only if it closes on the
  same line. Without it the regex `/case\s*(?:'|")/` — which `tier-single-source.test.ts` really
  contains — opens a "string" that swallows the rest of the file, hiding code and turning comments
  into string content. There is a control for that too.

**`test/policy-fail-open.test.ts` (new)** — TC-INT-06, the M-1 guard, **moved** out of
`blockscout-fallback.test.ts` because `vi.mock` is file-global. Left in place it would have made the
policy throw for every test in that file, and fail-open would have turned each throw into "satisfying"
— four cases needing a real `false` (including the H-1 regression) would have failed while the pairing
case passed **vacuously**. The throwing predicate is produced by mocking the class dictionary, where
the entry **resolves successfully and the predicate throws when CALLED**; had the resolution thrown,
construction would have failed and there would be nothing left to observe. The file builds its OWN
route table and duplicates five helpers rather than extracting them (PLAN §0.4 radius).

**Rejected: a sixth constructor parameter** (an injectable class map). `data-model.md` §M-6 decided
only the manifest map is injected, and overruling it here would be re-deciding an approved
architecture item without the architect. **But M-6's stated rationale — "no scenario needs a
DIFFERENT policy dictionary" — is refuted by this very file**, which needs a predicate that throws.
The counter-example is handed to **012-10** for `open-questions.md`, not silently worked around.

**`test/blockscout-fallback.test.ts` — one conversion, one removal.** `:315` became
`policy: { kind: 'someElementHasAny', fields: ['labels'] }`; the field list is `labels` ALONE and not
the production route's three, because the mutation "apply route B's policy to route A's adapter" is
only detectable by a policy blockscout's one-entry empty answer FAILS. This file mocks nothing.

**Every guard was verified by mutation, with the failure text recorded**: the banned identifier
introduced as an export (`packages/core/src/adapters/policy.ts: identifier nonEmpty`) and as an
alias in a test file (`packages/core/test/policy-fail-open.test.ts: identifier nonEmpty`) — both
red, both reverted); `try/catch` removed from `satisfies()` → TC-INT-06 alone dies with
`CapabilityUnavailableError: … tried: blockscout (policy bug), nansen (policy bug)`, which is M-1
verbatim — the policy's own message blamed on the providers; the policy taken from "whichever route
declared one first" → only the M-2 pairing case dies (`expected 'nansen' to be 'blockscout'`); the
two validation steps swapped → both TC-INT-02 cases die; the two loops merged → only its second case
dies.

## T-012 task 012-7 (2026-08-04) — the deadline's NETWORK layer: two signals per hop, two typed refusals in the limiter

The bottom layer of ADR-002 **D4**, built with **no consumer above it** (`resolve()` starts passing
a deadline in 012-8) and therefore proved in isolation. `deadlineAtMs` is everywhere an **ABSOLUTE
epoch-ms moment**, never a duration: a duration has to be re-derived by every step that forwards it,
and that is precisely how each of the up-to-4 redirect hops used to receive a fresh FULL timeout.

**`src/net/safe-fetch.ts`** — `SafeFetchOptions.deadlineAtMs?`, the new `DeadlineExceededError`, and
a hop loop with two mechanisms instead of one:

- **Entry pre-check.** A remainder `<= 0` throws before any network call. It is a DUPLICATE of the
  signal below and stays: a signal can only abort a call already issued, and not issuing it is the
  whole point.
- **Two signals, and the class is chosen by WHICH ONE FIRED.** `AbortSignal.any` reports that one of
  its inputs aborted and never which; reading `deadlineSignal.aborted` inside the composed handler
  answers "has it aborted BY NOW", whose answer depends on timer construction order. So each input
  gets its own listener and the first to fire writes the verdict (`composeHopAbort`). Per the DOM
  spec a source signal's `abort` event fires before its dependent signals' (signal abort, steps 5
  then 6), so the verdict is always written before the composed handler reads it.
  `deadlineSignal` → `DeadlineExceededError`; the caller's signal → **its own reason, unwrapped**;
  otherwise → `SafeFetchTimeoutError`.
- **`effectiveHopMs = timeoutMs`, UNCLAMPED — a decision, not an omission.** Clamping to
  `min(timeoutMs, remaining)` makes both timers expire in the same millisecond, `hopSignal` is
  constructed first and wins every tie, and `DeadlineExceededError` is then never born — C-1 one
  floor down, ending traversals in the `CapabilityUnavailableError` that OD-4/R-145 forbid. The
  remainder is already carried by `deadlineSignal`; clamping buys nothing and costs the
  discriminator. **Pinned by TC-UNIT-06a, which is deliberately ASYMMETRIC** (deadline 25 ms, hop
  timeout 50 ms): at an exact tie `min(t,t) = t` and both implementations answer identically, so the
  tie cannot tell them apart.
- **The caller's `opts.signal` stops being overwritten** (H1). It used to be discarded by
  `{...opts, redirect:'manual', signal}` — not merely absent, ignored.
- **Untouched by design:** `assertAllowedHost` on every hop, the size cap, `capResponseStream`,
  cross-host header stripping, the non-`https` refusals. Without a deadline and without a caller
  signal there is ONE input signal and it is passed through unwrapped — the previous behaviour
  exactly, which is what lets the 30 pre-existing cases stand unedited.

**`src/net/rate-limit.ts`** — `throttle(providerId, config, weight?, deadlineAtMs?)` and
`DeadlineWouldExceedError`, a sibling of `RateLimitRejectedError`. **Two conditions, never one:**
`remainingMs <= 0` is genuine expiry, true for every adapter on the route → `DeadlineExceededError`
with no wait at all; `remainingMs > 0` with `remainingMs - waitMs < MIN_POST_WAIT_REMAINDER_MS`
(5 000 ms, the shortest per-hop `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` any adapter configures) is a different fact —
not through THIS bucket, ask the next provider → `DeadlineWouldExceedError`. **The test is on what
the wait LEAVES** (adversarial cycle 2, F-2): the first version compared `waitMs > remainingMs`,
which admitted the equality and every wait leaving a sliver — the caller slept out its budget and
`safeFetch` then produced the TERMINAL `DeadlineExceededError`, cancelling every adapter behind the
saturated one, i.e. the defect this branch exists to prevent. Merging them is H-1 one floor down:
a burst saturating free `blockscout` (`capacity 5, refillPerSec 2`) would end the traversal before
`nansen`, whose bucket is idle and for whom the remaining seconds are real. Both refuse **after** the
reservation and refund it (`bucket.tokens += weight`), like the `MAX_WAIT_MS` branch above them; both
read the SAME `nowMs` sample as the refill and the spend, so no `await` enters the synchronous prefix
and the concurrency guarantee is untouched. The fairness cap keeps its precedence, so its class and
message are byte-for-byte what they were (pinned by string equality in TC-UNIT-12).

**Placement note:** the deadline branches live on the DEFICIT path, so a caller with a spent deadline
and a full bucket is still admitted — a refund is only meaningful where a reservation was made, and
`safeFetch`'s own entry check refuses one layer down, at the layer that would make the call.

**`tdd-strict` (PLAN §0.9): 16 new cases, 12 of them RED first, each by its declared
`EXPECTED_FAIL_REASON`** — 8 resolved instead of rejecting (the parameter was declared and ignored),
4 rejected with the wrong class (`SafeFetchTimeoutError` where the deadline or the caller owned the
cancellation). **4 were green from the first run and are labelled so in place**: TC-UNIT-05,
TC-UNIT-07 (×2) and TC-UNIT-12 assert behaviour that must SURVIVE the change, so no state of the tree
makes them red — their power is shown by mutation instead, not by a red-green cycle.

**Mutation-verified, each reverted byte-for-byte** (`diff` against a pre-mutation copy): clamping
`effectiveHopMs` → TC-UNIT-06a dies with `expected SafeFetchTimeoutError … to be an instance of
DeadlineExceededError` (TC-UNIT-03 and TC-UNIT-06 with it; TC-UNIT-05 survives, as its docstring
claims); dropping the source discriminator for a bare `AbortSignal.any` → TC-UNIT-03/04/06/06a die,
TC-UNIT-01/02 survive on the pre-check; merging the limiter's two branches → TC-UNIT-10 dies with
`expected DeadlineExceededError … to be an instance of DeadlineWouldExceedError`; removing both
`remainingMs <= 0` pre-checks → TC-UNIT-02 and TC-UNIT-09 die (on the transport side the mutation
lets the call go out and RESOLVE; on the limiter side no wait happens either, but the refusal is
MISLABELLED `DeadlineWouldExceedError`, which is the harm — the registry would never set
`deadlineHit`); hoisting `assertAllowedHost` out of the loop → TC-UNIT-07's first case dies.

**Test mechanics, and why they are not the usual ones.** `safeFetch` gets **no injectable clock** —
that would be a FOURTH seam where PLAN §0.3 names three, and OD-6 fixed their extent by name. The
mechanism is a fake `fetchImpl` plus a TINY REAL timeout (the precedent at `safe-fetch.test.ts:138`),
5-60 ms per case, because `AbortSignal.timeout` runs on Node's timers, which vitest's fake timers do
not move — and mocking `AbortSignal` would leave the test with no input on which it could fail. Every
racing case keeps its two timers ≥2.5x apart; the one case that must land BETWEEN two hops blocks the
event loop rather than sleeping on it, so no timer can fire mid-hop and "exactly 2 calls" is not a
scheduler coin flip. The limiter cases are entirely on virtual clocks (`createThrottle({now, wait})`)
— PLAN §0.7's residual requirement after WI-26 closed, and `throttle-seam.test.ts` stays green (13
cases).

## T-012 task 012-8 (2026-08-04) — the deadline's REGISTRY layer: one moment, computed once, and a third typed outcome

The consumer 012-7 was built for. `CapabilityRegistry.resolve(capability, chain, args,
requestedDeadlineAtMs?)` now derives ONE absolute moment and threads it, unchanged, into every
`adapter.fetch(cap, args, deadlineAtMs?)` of the walk.

**`src/adapters/types.ts`** — `ProviderAdapter.fetch` takes an optional third parameter. **Optional
is the requirement, not a convenience (R-140e):** 11 of the 12 adapters ignore it after this task and
are not broken by it — they degrade to their own per-hop timeout while the registry still refuses
every adapter it has not yet reached. Only `blockscout` reads it here; `nansen` follows in 012-9,
where a paid leg makes the boundary a correctness condition. **In the SHIPPED tree the number is 10,
not 11** (adversarial cycle 2, F-7: the 012-8 count was being read as the current state in four
places) — and by CAPABILITY the ceiling is enforced on 4 of 20 (WI-37, and the ENFORCEMENT section
of `capability-manifest.ts`).
**SUPERSEDED 2026-08-05 by WI-37:** ten adapters read it and two do not, both of them stubs that
never run; the ceiling is enforced on 20 of 20. R-140e itself is unchanged and still tested — see
TC-INT-07's docstring for why the case stays after its population went to zero.

**`src/adapters/registry.ts`** — four pieces, each with a decision behind it:

- **`effectiveDeadlineAtMs = min(nowMs + manifest.deadlineMs, requested ?? Infinity)`, computed
  ONCE** and passed through (the budget gate's `dayBucketMs` discipline). One `Date.now()` sample
  feeds the validation, the refusal and the `min()`, so they cannot disagree about "now".
- **TWO forms of a bad request, deliberately not folded into one (L-1).** `!Number.isSafeInteger(x)`
  → read as ABSENCE, because `Math.min(a, NaN)` is `NaN` and `NaN <= 0` is `false`: the harm is not a
  wrong deadline but an INERT one that no later comparison can act on. A well-formed moment already
  in the past → **immediate `CapabilityDeadlineExceededError` with `tried: []`**, never "absence":
  absence means the full manifest budget, i.e. MORE time than was asked for, and R-144 forbids
  widening in either direction. (Clamping to `now()` was rejected upstream: for one tick it makes an
  expired deadline indistinguishable from an absent one.)
- **The pre-check is the FIRST statement of the loop body — ABOVE the cache read**, and that is a
  product decision recorded, not a detail: a deadline means "after this moment we do not answer", not
  "after this moment we do not go to the network", which is a distinction the caller cannot predict.
  Refusing to SERVE a cached entry is not touching it — nothing evicts or negative-writes, so the
  next call inside budget still gets it (TC-INT-09 and TC-INT-10 assert the two directions
  separately). **Time is its only condition**; ORing in "some earlier adapter threw something
  deadline-flavoured" would let one provider's saturation skip every provider behind it.
- **OD-4: expiry sets `hadFailure` UNCONDITIONALLY, alongside `deadlineHit`.** Without it, H-1's
  `if (unsatisfying && !hadFailure) return unsatisfying;` publishes the answer of whoever replied
  before the clock ran out — "Nansen answered: no labels" where the truth is "Nansen was never
  asked". Terminal branch: `if (deadlineHit || Date.now() >= effectiveDeadlineAtMs) throw new
CapabilityDeadlineExceededError(...)`, whose `tried[]` names BOTH groups.

**The second disjunct is LOAD-BEARING TODAY, not a guard for a hypothetical future adapter — and it
had no test.** Its comment in `registry.ts` (inherited from the architecture) described it as
belt-and-braces against "a future adapter whose error handling swallows the typed error". Measured
during 012-8's mandated mutation runs: with `|| Date.now() >= effectiveDeadlineAtMs` deleted, **all
1404 tests still passed**. The swallowing adapter is `blockscout`, in this same commit — M-7 makes it
re-message every transport throw BY CLASS NAME (`safeFetch` interpolates a full URL into three of its
own errors, and this is the one adapter authenticating with `?apikey=` inside that URL), so the typed
class survives only on `.cause` and `deadlineHit` is never set for it. **TC-INT-11 was added beyond
the task's enumerated list** to hold the branch down, driven through the REAL adapter rather than a
fake that would merely encode one reading of its catch. The alternative — unwrapping
`DeadlineExceededError` in `blockscout` — is behaviour change outside 012-8's stated uptake and fixes
one adapter where the disjunct covers all of them; raised rather than taken.

**Filed as [WI-36](../../docs/backlog/wi-36-adapter-wrappers-flatten-typed-errors.md)**, because a
defect recorded only in two code comments and a test docstring is not findable by anyone asking
"which adapters flatten typed errors on the way out?". The work-item carries the measurement that
answers it: **two of twelve** adapters wrap a `safeFetch` throw into a new `Error`
(`blockscout/index.ts:368`, `blockchain-info/index.ts:167`), `rpc-evm`/`rpc-solana` rethrow the
original as itself, and the remaining eight do not wrap at all. `blockchain-info` is the **latent
second instance** — the identical wrapper, on an adapter that does not read the deadline yet, so it
would go live silently. The terminal branch now points at WI-36 rather than being the only record of
its own necessity, and states that it stays after WI-36 lands: twelve adapters covered versus two
fixed.
**CLOSED 2026-08-05 (WI-36 + WI-37, same commit):** both wrappers now rethrow the listed transport
classes unwrapped, and `blockchain-info` took the deadline up in the same commit, so the latent
instance never went live. The disjunct stayed, exactly as argued; TC-INT-11 split into a real-adapter
case and a fake-adapter one, because no shipped adapter is in the flattening shape any more.
